After returning from a stimulating conference and short art‑focused vacation, he threw himself into a new program at his clinic, taking on extra groups, staying up late to design novel interventions, and cutting back on the routines—like regular hikes and quiet evenings with his spouse—that usually grounded him. When a trusted colleague gently confronted him about a risky clinical decision and suggested his thinking seemed unlike him, Garrett—distressed by the realization that he could no longer trust his own judgment—agreed to a formal psychiatric evaluation.\n- Chief Complaint: “My thoughts feel like they’re racing nonstop, jumping from idea to idea so fast that I can’t keep my head clear, even in sessions.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S020 (Hallucinations): Lately in my office I’ve been hearing what I’m convinced is a woman humming a specific tune and occasionally saying my complex case formulation—have started to feel oddly flat and optional, like I’m just going through the motions without that sense of pleasure or engagement I usually count on to center me.\n- S026 (Weight_Appetite_Change): Over the past few weeks I’ve basically stopped feeling hungry at all, skipping meals without noticing until my wife comments on how loose my clothes are and I realize I’ve lost enough weight that even my work slacks are hanging off me.\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I’m lying in bed wide awake until three or four in the morning, feeling wired rather than tired, and when I do finally drift off it’s light and choppy so I’m up again before dawn with my mind already racing through ideas for the clinic.\n- S028 (Psychomotor_Changes): My wife keeps pointing out that I can’t seem to sit still anymore—instead of relaxing on the couch I’m constantly pacing the hallway, fiddling with pens, or jumping up mid-conversation to reorganize something or jot down a new idea that suddenly seems urgent.\n- S029 (Fatigue): Even though I’m moving a lot and talking a lot, underneath it I feel this bone-deep tiredness, like I’ve been running a mental marathon for sense that I’ve completely failed my patients and my team—that by making these grand plans and half-baked changes at the clinic I’ve not only embarrassed myself but actually harmed people, and that I don’t deserve to call myself a psychologist anymore.\n- S032 (Death_Suicide): On the worst days I catch myself thinking that it might be a relief if I just didn’t wake up, and I’ve had a few moments driving home where the idea of not having to wrestle with my brain anymore feels disturbingly appealing, even though I haven’t made any concrete plans to hurt myself.\n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): I swing between feeling almost euphoric and invincible—like I’m on the verge of a breakthrough that will revolutionize our clinic—and getting irrationally snappy with my wife or colleagues over tiny things, such as them questioning one of my new ideas or asking me to slow down.\n- S034 (Increased_Activity): I’ve been taking on extra groups, rewriting treatment manuals at 2 a.m., starting new art projects, and rearranging the house, all at once, because I feel this relentless drive to keep producing and can’t tolerate the idea of just sitting quietly or doing nothing.\n- S037 (Pressured_Speech): In sessions and meetings I hear myself talking almost nonstop, jumping in before people finish their sentences, speaking faster and louder than usual, and even when I see my clients or colleagues looking overwhelmed I feel this internal pressure that won’t let me pause or stay silent.\n- S038 (Racing_Thoughts): It feels like there’s a runaway train in my head—ideas about clients, new protocols, paintings, song lyrics, and odd symbolic connections all colliding at once so that even in session I’m leaping from one association to another and struggling to hold onto a single coherent line of thought.\n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): I’ll be in the middle of a serious conversation with a patient and suddenly find my attention pulled away by the pattern in their shirt, a noise in the hallway, or a line from a song that pops into my head, and before I know it I’m off on a tangent that has little to do with what they actually came in to talk about.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- delusions_and_hallucinations_occur_exclusively_during_the_manic_episode: The humming voice and the sense that certain songs or artworks are giving me special instructions only started once my mood ramped up and my sleep fell apart after that conference, and they fade away completely during the more drained, low stretches when I’m just feeling guilty and exhausted.\n- functional_impairment: In the past month I’ve had to cancel or transfer several patients after my supervisor raised concerns about my judgment, my clinic director has pulled me from leading the new program I created, and at home my wife says she feels like she’s living with a stranger who’s always working, pacing, or arguing instead of being present with her.\n- duration: Looking back, this whole episode—from the initial buzz after the conference through the crash into this heavy, guilty state—has been unfolding over the past few months rather than just a bad week or two.\n- depressive_episode_duration: The flattened mood, loss of interest in my usual outlets, and the darker thoughts about not wanting to wake up have been hanging over me for roughly the last four It was incredibly unsettling to experience as a psychologist, but in those highly wired moments, I felt convinced the voice was guiding me on what to do next with my patients. This only occurred when my mood was ramped up and my sleep had collapsed, disappearing completely once I crashed into this low state."

Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm a psychologist, and I've come in for an evaluation because my thoughts feel like they’re racing nonstop, jumping from idea to idea so fast that I can’t keep my head clear, even in sessions with my patients. It's deeply unsettling because I've always prided myself on careful self-monitoring, but right now I feel like I'm losing my cognitive grip.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been experiencing periods of persistent irritability, elevated mood, or an increased sense of energy along with these racing thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been swinging between feeling almost euphoric and invincible, and then getting irrationally snappy with my wife and colleagues over minor things. At the same time, I feel this relentless drive to keep producing, taking on extra clinic work and starting multiple projects at all hours because I just can't sit still.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how long you've been experiencing these episodes of racing thoughts, elevated mood, and increased energy, and whether they come and go or have been persistent over time?\n\nPatient: This entire experience has been unfolding over the past few months, but it has shifted. The intense, high-energy surge with the racing thoughts and elevated mood was a temporary phase that has since passed, whereas this heavier, flatter, and guilt-ridden state has been persistently hanging over me for about the last four months.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if there were distinct periods where you experienced the high-energy, elevated mood state without the depressive symptoms, and if so, how long those periods typically lasted before shifting to the current state?\n\nPatient: Yes, there was a very distinct, highly driven period that started right after I returned from a stimulating conference, and that intense, elevated state lasted for about a month without any depression. Once that surge faded, I transitioned directly into this heavy, flat state which has now been dominant for the last four months.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many days or weeks the elevated mood and high-energy state lasted before you transitioned into the depressive state, and have you experienced any similar episodes of elevated mood in the past?\n\nPatient: The elevated, high-energy phase lasted for about a month after I returned from my conference, right before I transitioned into this depressive state that has now been going on for about four months. I have never experienced an episode like this in the past, as I've always prided myself on maintaining a careful balance and self-monitoring.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the month-long period of elevated mood and high energy was completely free of depressive symptoms, and did it meet a full two weeks of duration with at least three symptoms of mania or hypomania?\n\nPatient: Actually, looking back, that high-energy month wasn't completely free of depressive symptoms; those flat feelings and dark thoughts have been looming for about four months, overlapping even as that surge came and went. That intense phase lasted well over two weeks—about a month—and definitely involved multiple symptoms like my racing thoughts, pressured speech, insomnia, and taking on far too many activities.\n\nDoctor: Did the month-long period of elevated mood and high energy significantly impact your daily life or relationships in a noticeable way, such as requiring hospitalization or causing problems at work or with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has severely disrupted both my career and my marriage. At the clinic, I was pulled from leading my new program and had to transfer several patients after concerns were raised about my clinical judgment. At home, my wife feels like she is living with a stranger who is constantly working, pacing, or arguing instead of being present with her.\n\nDoctor: Can you further clarify if there were any periods where you experienced depressive symptoms concurrently with the manic or hypomanic symptoms, or if these states occurred distinctly separate from one another?\n\nPatient: The states actually overlapped significantly, as the low mood, loss of interest, and darker thoughts have been present for about four months, persisting even during that high-energy surge. Even while running on high energy, I felt a bone-deep fatigue underneath and intense guilt if I slowed down for a moment, though the humming voice I heard only occurred during those peak manic periods.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing a humming voice during the peak manic periods, can you tell me more about this experience, such as what the voice said or how it made you feel?\n\nPatient: In my closed office, I would hear what sounded like a woman humming a specific tune and occasionally saying my name, and it felt like an actual, physical sound in the room rather than just a thought. It was incredibly unsettling to experience as a psychologist, but in those highly wired moments, I felt convinced the voice was guiding me on what to do next with my patients. This only occurred when my mood was ramped up and my sleep had collapsed, disappearing completely once I crashed into this low state.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the humming voice you heard during the manic periods was the only psychotic symptom you experienced, or were there any other hallucinations or delusions present during that time or at any other point?\n\nPatient: No, the humming voice wasn't the only thing; I also felt convinced that I was receiving special insights and instructions from artwork and song lyrics to guide my treatment decisions. Both the voice and those symbolic insights occurred exclusively during those highly wired, sleep-deprived manic periods. Once I crashed into this lower, depressed state, those experiences completely disappeared."

That intense phase lasted well over two weeks—about a month—and definitely involved multiple symptoms like my racing thoughts, pressured speech, insomnia, and taking on far too many activities.\n\nDoctor: Did the month-long period of elevated mood and high energy significantly impact your daily life or relationships in a noticeable way, such as requiring hospitalization or causing problems at work or with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has severely disrupted both my career and my marriage. At the clinic, I was pulled from leading my new program and had to transfer several patients after concerns were raised about my clinical judgment. At home, my wife feels like she is living with a stranger who is constantly working, pacing, or arguing instead of being present with her.\n\nDoctor: Can you further clarify if there were any periods where you experienced depressive symptoms concurrently with the manic or hypomanic symptoms, or if these states occurred distinctly separate from one another?\n\nPatient: The states actually overlapped significantly, as the low mood, loss of interest, and darker thoughts have been present for about four months, persisting even during that high-energy surge. Even while running on high energy, I felt a bone-deep fatigue underneath and intense guilt if I slowed down for a moment, though the humming voice I heard only occurred during those peak manic periods.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing a humming voice during the peak manic periods, can you tell me more about this experience, such as what the voice said or how it made you feel?\n\nPatient: In my closed office, I would hear what sounded like a woman humming a specific tune and occasionally saying my name, and it felt like an actual, physical sound in the room rather than just a thought. It was incredibly unsettling to experience as a psychologist, but in those highly wired moments, I felt convinced the voice was guiding me on what to do next with my patients. This only occurred when my mood was ramped up and my sleep had collapsed, disappearing completely once I crashed into this low state.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the humming voice you heard during the manic periods was the only psychotic symptom you experienced, or were there any other hallucinations or delusions present during that time or at any other point?\n\nPatient: No, the humming voice wasn't the only thing; I also felt convinced that I was receiving special insights and instructions from artwork and song lyrics to guide my treatment decisions. Both the voice and those symbolic insights occurred exclusively during those highly wired, sleep-deprived manic periods. Once I crashed into this lower, depressed state, those experiences completely disappeared.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever lose touch with reality or have difficulty distinguishing between the humming voice, special insights, and your own thoughts during the manic periods, or were you always aware that these experiences were unusual and not part of everyday reality?\n\nPatient: During those manic periods, I completely lost touch with reality and genuinely believed those experiences were real, feeling convinced that the humming was an actual sound in the room and that the insights from art were truly guiding my clinical decisions. It was only after a trusted colleague confronted me about a risky decision and my mood began to shift that I had the distressing realization that I had lost touch with reality and could no longer trust my own judgment."

At home, my wife feels like she is living with a stranger who is constantly working, pacing, or arguing instead of being present with her.\n\nDoctor: Can you further clarify if there were any periods where you experienced depressive symptoms concurrently with the manic or hypomanic symptoms, or if these states occurred distinctly separate from one another?\n\nPatient: The states actually overlapped significantly, as the low mood, loss of interest, and darker thoughts have been present for about four months, persisting even during that high-energy surge. Even while running on high energy, I felt a bone-deep fatigue underneath and intense guilt if I slowed down for a moment, though the humming voice I heard only occurred during those peak manic periods.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing a humming voice during the peak manic periods, can you tell me more about this experience, such as what the voice said or how it made you feel?\n\nPatient: In my closed office, I would hear what sounded like a woman humming a specific tune and occasionally saying my name, and it felt like an actual, physical sound in the room rather than just a thought. It was incredibly unsettling to experience as a psychologist, but in those highly wired moments, I felt convinced the voice was guiding me on what to do next with my patients. This only occurred when my mood was ramped up and my sleep had collapsed, disappearing completely once I crashed into this low state.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the humming voice you heard during the manic periods was the only psychotic symptom you experienced, or were there any other hallucinations or delusions present during that time or at any other point?\n\nPatient: No, the humming voice wasn't the only thing; I also felt convinced that I was receiving special insights and instructions from artwork and song lyrics to guide my treatment decisions. Both the voice and those symbolic insights occurred exclusively during those highly wired, sleep-deprived manic periods. Once I crashed into this lower, depressed state, those experiences completely disappeared.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever lose touch with reality or have difficulty distinguishing between the humming voice, special insights, and your own thoughts during the manic periods, or were you always aware that these experiences were unusual and not part of everyday reality?\n\nPatient: During those manic periods, I completely lost touch with reality and genuinely believed those experiences were real, feeling convinced that the humming was an actual sound in the room and that the insights from art were truly guiding my clinical decisions. It was only after a trusted colleague confronted me about a risky decision and my mood began to shift that I had the distressing realization that I had lost touch with reality and could no longer trust my own judgment.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if the depressive symptoms you've been experiencing for the last four months have included any psychotic symptoms, such as hallucinations or delusions, similar to those you experienced during the manic periods?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, those psychotic symptoms have not occurred at all during the depressive periods. They only happen when my mood is highly ramped up and my sleep is completely disrupted. During these low, exhausted, and guilty stretches, the voice and the special insights disappear completely.

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm a psychologist, and I've come in for an evaluation because my thoughts feel like they’re racing nonstop, jumping from idea to idea so fast that I can’t keep my head clear, even in sessions with my patients. It's deeply unsettling because I've always prided myself on careful self-monitoring, but right now I feel like I'm losing my cognitive grip.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been experiencing periods of persistent irritability, elevated mood, or an increased sense of energy along with these racing thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been swinging between feeling almost euphoric and invincible, and then getting irrationally snappy with my wife and colleagues over minor things. At the same time, I feel this relentless drive to keep producing, taking on extra clinic work and starting multiple projects at all hours because I just can't sit still.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how long you've been experiencing these episodes of racing thoughts, elevated mood, and increased energy, and whether they come and go or have been persistent over time?\n\nPatient: This entire experience has been unfolding over the past few months, but it has shifted. The intense, high-energy surge with the racing thoughts and elevated mood was a temporary phase that has since passed, whereas this heavier, flatter, and guilt-ridden state has been persistently hanging over me for about the last four months.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if there were distinct periods where you experienced the high-energy, elevated mood state without the depressive symptoms, and if so, how long those periods typically lasted before shifting to the current state?\n\nPatient: Yes, there was a very distinct, highly driven period that started right after I returned from a stimulating conference, and that intense, elevated state lasted for about a month without any depression. Once that surge faded, I transitioned directly into this heavy, flat state which has now been dominant for the last four months.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many days or weeks the elevated mood and high-energy state lasted before you transitioned into the depressive state, and have you experienced any similar episodes of elevated mood in the past?\n\nPatient: The elevated, high-energy phase lasted for about a month after I returned from my conference, right before I transitioned into this depressive state that has now been going on for about four months. I have never experienced an episode like this in the past, as I've always prided myself on maintaining a careful balance and self-monitoring.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the month-long period of elevated mood and high energy was completely free of depressive symptoms, and did it meet a full two weeks of duration with at least three symptoms of mania or hypomania?\n\nPatient: Actually, looking back, that high-energy month wasn't completely free of depressive symptoms; those flat feelings and dark thoughts have been looming for about four months, overlapping even as that surge came and went. That intense phase lasted well over two weeks—about a month—and definitely involved multiple symptoms like my racing thoughts, pressured speech, insomnia, and taking on far too many activities.\n\nDoctor: Did the month-long period of elevated mood and high energy significantly impact your daily life or relationships in a noticeable way, such as requiring hospitalization or causing problems at work or with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has severely disrupted both my career and my marriage. At the clinic, I was pulled from leading my new program and had to transfer several patients after concerns were raised about my clinical judgment. At home, my wife feels like she is living with a stranger who is constantly working, pacing, or arguing instead of being present with her.\n\nDoctor: Can you further clarify if there were any periods where you experienced depressive symptoms concurrently with the manic or hypomanic symptoms, or if these states occurred distinctly separate from one another?\n\nPatient: The states actually overlapped significantly, as the low mood, loss of interest, and darker thoughts have been present for about four months, persisting even during that high-energy surge. Even while running on high energy, I felt a bone-deep fatigue underneath and intense guilt if I slowed down for a moment, though the humming voice I heard only occurred during those peak manic periods.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing a humming voice during the peak manic periods, can you tell me more about this experience, such as what the voice said or how it made you feel?\n\nPatient: In my closed office, I would hear what sounded like a woman humming a specific tune and occasionally saying my name, and it felt like an actual, physical sound in the room rather than just a thought. It was incredibly unsettling to experience as a psychologist, but in those highly wired moments, I felt convinced the voice was guiding me on what to do next with my patients. This only occurred when my mood was ramped up and my sleep had collapsed, disappearing completely once I crashed into this low state.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the humming voice you heard during the manic periods was the only psychotic symptom you experienced, or were there any other hallucinations or delusions present during that time or at any other point?\n\nPatient: No, the humming voice wasn't the only thing; I also felt convinced that I was receiving special insights and instructions from artwork and song lyrics to guide my treatment decisions. Both the voice and those symbolic insights occurred exclusively during those highly wired, sleep-deprived manic periods. Once I crashed into this lower, depressed state, those experiences completely disappeared.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever lose touch with reality or have difficulty distinguishing between the humming voice, special insights, and your own thoughts during the manic periods, or were you always aware that these experiences were unusual and not part of everyday reality?\n\nPatient: During those manic periods, I completely lost touch with reality and genuinely believed those experiences were real, feeling convinced that the humming was an actual sound in the room and that the insights from art were truly guiding my clinical decisions. It was only after a trusted colleague confronted me about a risky decision and my mood began to shift that I had the distressing realization that I had lost touch with reality and could no longer trust my own judgment.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if the depressive symptoms you've been experiencing for the last four months have included any psychotic symptoms, such as hallucinations or delusions, similar to those you experienced during the manic periods?\n\nPatient: No, those psychotic symptoms have not occurred at all during the depressive periods. They only happen when my mood is highly ramped up and my sleep is completely disrupted. During these low, exhausted, and guilty stretches, the voice and the special insights disappear completely.\n\nDoctor: Did the presence of psychotic symptoms, such as the humming voice and special insights, only occur during the manic or hypomanic episodes, or were they also present during any periods of depression?\n\nPatient: Yes, the humming voice and the special insights only occurred during the high-energy, wired periods when my mood was ramped up and my sleep fell apart. They faded away completely during the low, exhausted, and guilty stretches, and have not been present at all during my depression.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that the psychotic symptoms, like the humming voice and special insights, only occurred during the manic periods and not during the depressive episodes. Can you confirm if these psychotic symptoms were present for the majority of the time during those manic periods, or were they episodic and occurring only at the peak of your manic symptoms?"

Once that surge faded, I transitioned directly into this heavy, flat state which has now been dominant for the last four months.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many days or weeks the elevated mood and high-energy state lasted before you transitioned into the depressive state, and have you experienced any similar episodes of elevated mood in the past?\n\nPatient: The elevated, high-energy phase lasted for about a month after I returned from my conference, right before I transitioned into this depressive state that has now been going on for about four months. I have never experienced an episode like this in the past, as I've always prided myself on maintaining a careful balance and self-monitoring.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the month-long period of elevated mood and high energy was completely free of depressive symptoms, and did it meet a full two weeks of duration with at least three symptoms of mania or hypomania?\n\nPatient: Actually, looking back, that high-energy month wasn't completely free of depressive symptoms; those flat feelings and dark thoughts have been looming for about four months, overlapping even as that surge came and went. That intense phase lasted well over two weeks—about a month—and definitely involved multiple symptoms like my racing thoughts, pressured speech, insomnia, and taking on far too many activities.\n\nDoctor: Did the month-long period of elevated mood and high energy significantly impact your daily life or relationships in a noticeable way, such as requiring hospitalization or causing problems at work or with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has severely disrupted both my career and my marriage. At the clinic, I was pulled from leading my new program and had to transfer several patients after concerns were raised about my clinical judgment. At home, my wife feels like she is living with a stranger who is constantly working, pacing, or arguing instead of being present with her.\n\nDoctor: Can you further clarify if there were any periods where you experienced depressive symptoms concurrently with the manic or hypomanic symptoms, or if these states occurred distinctly separate from one another?\n\nPatient: The states actually overlapped significantly, as the low mood, loss of interest, and darker thoughts have been present for about four months, persisting even during that high-energy surge. Even while running on high energy, I felt a bone-deep fatigue underneath and intense guilt if I slowed down for a moment, though the humming voice I heard only occurred during those peak manic periods.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing a humming voice during the peak manic periods, can you tell me more about this experience, such as what the voice said or how it made you feel?\n\nPatient: In my closed office, I would hear what sounded like a woman humming a specific tune and occasionally saying my name, and it felt like an actual, physical sound in the room rather than just a thought. It was incredibly unsettling to experience as a psychologist, but in those highly wired moments, I felt convinced the voice was guiding me on what to do next with my patients. This only occurred when my mood was ramped up and my sleep had collapsed, disappearing completely once I crashed into this low state.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the humming voice you heard during the manic periods was the only psychotic symptom you experienced, or were there any other hallucinations or delusions present during that time or at any other point?\n\nPatient: No, the humming voice wasn't the only thing; I also felt convinced that I was receiving special insights and instructions from artwork and song lyrics to guide my treatment decisions. Both the voice and those symbolic insights occurred exclusively during those highly wired, sleep-deprived manic periods. Once I crashed into this lower, depressed state, those experiences completely disappeared.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever lose touch with reality or have difficulty distinguishing between the humming voice, special insights, and your own thoughts during the manic periods, or were you always aware that these experiences were unusual and not part of everyday reality?\n\nPatient: During those manic periods, I completely lost touch with reality and genuinely believed those experiences were real, feeling convinced that the humming was an actual sound in the room and that the insights from art were truly guiding my clinical decisions. It was only after a trusted colleague confronted me about a risky decision and my mood began to shift that I had the distressing realization that I had lost touch with reality and could no longer trust my own judgment.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if the depressive symptoms you've been experiencing for the last four months have included any psychotic symptoms, such as hallucinations or delusions, similar to those you experienced during the manic periods?\n\nPatient: No, those psychotic symptoms have not occurred at all during the depressive periods. They only happen when my mood is highly ramped up and my sleep is completely disrupted. During these low, exhausted, and guilty stretches, the voice and the special insights disappear completely.\n\nDoctor: Did the presence of psychotic symptoms, such as the humming voice and special insights, only occur during the manic or hypomanic episodes, or were they also present during any periods of depression?\n\nPatient: Yes, the humming voice and the special insights only occurred during the high-energy, wired periods when my mood was ramped up and my sleep fell apart. They faded away completely during the low, exhausted, and guilty stretches, and have not been present at all during my depression.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that the psychotic symptoms, like the humming voice and special insights, only occurred during the manic periods and not during the depressive episodes. Can you confirm if these psychotic symptoms were present for the majority of the time during those manic periods, or were they episodic and occurring only at the peak of your manic symptoms?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure about the exact proportion of time. I can't really confirm if it was the majority of the time or just the absolute peak, but those experiences definitely seemed tied to when my mind was racing the fastest and my sleep was at its worst."
  }

Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm a psychologist, and I've come in for an evaluation because my thoughts feel like they’re racing nonstop, jumping from idea to idea so fast that I can’t keep my head clear, even in sessions with my patients. It's deeply unsettling because I've always prided myself on careful self-monitoring, but right now I feel like I'm losing my cognitive grip.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been experiencing periods of persistent irritability, elevated mood, or an increased sense of energy along with these racing thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been swinging between feeling almost euphoric and invincible, and then getting irrationally snappy with my wife and colleagues over minor things. At the same time, I feel this relentless drive to keep producing, taking on extra clinic work and starting multiple projects at all hours because I just can't sit still.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how long you've been experiencing these episodes of racing thoughts, elevated mood, and increased energy, and whether they come and go or have been persistent over time?\n\nPatient: This entire experience has been unfolding over the past few months, but it has shifted. The intense, high-energy surge with the racing thoughts and elevated mood was a temporary phase that has since passed, whereas this heavier, flatter, and guilt-ridden state has been persistently hanging over me for about the last four months.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if there were distinct periods where you experienced the high-energy, elevated mood state without the depressive symptoms, and if so, how long those periods typically lasted before shifting to the current state?\n\nPatient: Yes, there was a very distinct, highly driven period that started right after I returned from a stimulating conference, and that intense, elevated state lasted for about a month without any depression. Once that surge faded, I transitioned directly into this heavy, flat state which has now been dominant for the last four months.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many days or weeks the elevated mood and high-energy state lasted before you transitioned into the depressive state, and have you experienced any similar episodes of elevated mood in the past?\n\nPatient: The elevated, high-energy phase lasted for about a month after I returned from my conference, right before I transitioned into this depressive state that has now been going on for about four months. I have never experienced an episode like this in the past, as I've always prided myself on maintaining a careful balance and self-monitoring.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the month-long period of elevated mood and high energy was completely free of depressive symptoms, and did it meet a full two weeks of duration with at least three symptoms of mania or hypomania?\n\nPatient: Actually, looking back, that high-energy month wasn't completely free of depressive symptoms; those flat feelings and dark thoughts have been looming for about four months, overlapping even as that surge came and went. That intense phase lasted well over two weeks—about a month—and definitely involved multiple symptoms like my racing thoughts, pressured speech, insomnia, and taking on far too many activities.\n\nDoctor: Did the month-long period of elevated mood and high energy significantly impact your daily life or relationships in a noticeable way, such as requiring hospitalization or causing problems at work or with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has severely disrupted both my career and my marriage. At the clinic, I was pulled from leading my new program and had to transfer several patients after concerns were raised about my clinical judgment. At home, my wife feels like she is living with a stranger who is constantly working, pacing, or arguing instead of being present with her.\n\nDoctor: Can you further clarify if there were any periods where you experienced depressive symptoms concurrently with the manic or hypomanic symptoms, or if these states occurred distinctly separate from one another?\n\nPatient: The states actually overlapped significantly, as the low mood, loss of interest, and darker thoughts have been present for about four months, persisting even during that high-energy surge. Even while running on high energy, I felt a bone-deep fatigue underneath and intense guilt if I slowed down for a moment, though the humming voice I heard only occurred during those peak manic periods.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing a humming voice during the peak manic periods, can you tell me more about this experience, such as what the voice said or how it made you feel?\n\nPatient: In my closed office, I would hear what sounded like a woman humming a specific tune and occasionally saying my name, and it felt like an actual, physical sound in the room rather than just a thought. It was incredibly unsettling to experience as a psychologist, but in those highly wired moments, I felt convinced the voice was guiding me on what to do next with my patients. This only occurred when my mood was ramped up and my sleep had collapsed, disappearing completely once I crashed into this low state.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the humming voice you heard during the manic periods was the only psychotic symptom you experienced, or were there any other hallucinations or delusions present during that time or at any other point?\n\nPatient: No, the humming voice wasn't the only thing; I also felt convinced that I was receiving special insights and instructions from artwork and song lyrics to guide my treatment decisions. Both the voice and those symbolic insights occurred exclusively during those highly wired, sleep-deprived manic periods. Once I crashed into this lower, depressed state, those experiences completely disappeared.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever lose touch with reality or have difficulty distinguishing between the humming voice, special insights, and your own thoughts during the manic periods, or were you always aware that these experiences were unusual and not part of everyday reality?\n\nPatient: During those manic periods, I completely lost touch with reality and genuinely believed those experiences were real, feeling convinced that the humming was an actual sound in the room and that the insights from art were truly guiding my clinical decisions. It was only after a trusted colleague confronted me about a risky decision and my mood began to shift that I had the distressing realization that I had lost touch with reality and could no longer trust my own judgment.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if the depressive symptoms you've been experiencing for the last four months have included any psychotic symptoms, such as hallucinations or delusions, similar to those you experienced during the manic periods?\n\nPatient: No, those psychotic symptoms have not occurred at all during the depressive periods. They only happen when my mood is highly ramped up and my sleep is completely disrupted. During these low, exhausted, and guilty stretches, the voice and the special insights disappear completely.\n\nDoctor: Did the presence of psychotic symptoms, such as the humming voice and special insights, only occur during the manic or hypomanic episodes, or were they also present during any periods of depression?\n\nPatient: Yes, the humming voice and the special insights only occurred during the high-energy, wired periods when my mood was ramped up and my sleep fell apart. They faded away completely during the low, exhausted, and guilty stretches, and have not been present at all during my depression.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that the psychotic symptoms, like the humming voice and special insights, only occurred during the manic periods and not during the depressive episodes. Can you confirm if these psychotic symptoms were present for the majority of the time during those manic periods, or were they episodic and occurring only at the peak of your manic symptoms?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure about the exact proportion of time. I can't really confirm if it was the majority of the time or just the absolute peak, but those experiences definitely seemed tied to when my mind was racing the fastest and my sleep was at its worst.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the psychotic symptoms, such as the humming voice and special insights, were directly related to the intensity of your manic symptoms, and did they disappear completely when you transitioned into the depressive state?\n\nPatient: Yes, I can confirm that. The humming voice and those special insights only occurred when my mood was highly elevated and my sleep was at its worst. Once I transitioned into this low, drained, and guilty state, those experiences faded away completely."

me from leading the new program I created, and at home my wife says she feels like she’s living with a stranger who’s always working, pacing, or arguing instead of being present with her.\n- duration: Looking back, this whole episode—from the initial buzz after the conference through the crash into this heavy, guilty state—has been unfolding over the past few months rather than just a bad week or two.\n- depressive_episode_duration: The flattened mood, loss of interest in my usual outlets, and the darker thoughts about not wanting to wake up have been hanging over me for roughly the last four months, gradually tightening their grip even as the more intense manic surge came and went.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “My thoughts feel like they’re racing nonstop, jumping from idea to idea so fast that I can’t keep my head clear, even in sessions.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: Additional Diagnostic Requirements > delusions_and_hallucinations_occur_exclusively_during_the_manic_episode, Patient Background and Presenting Concern > Stressor / Background\nAnswer strategy: Acknowledge the doctor's question from your perspective as a psychologist. Confirm that the psychotic symptoms—like hearing the humming voice and finding special messages in artwork—only happen when you are highly wired and manic, and completely disappear when you are in a low, depressive state. Explain that, from a clinical standpoint, this distinction is crucial because it confirms the psychotic features are tied directly to the manic episodes (fitting Bipolar I) rather than indicating a continuous psychotic disorder, which helps you make sense of your diagnosis."

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm a psychologist, and I've come in for an evaluation because my thoughts feel like they’re racing nonstop, jumping from idea to idea so fast that I can’t keep my head clear, even in sessions with my patients. It's deeply unsettling because I've always prided myself on careful self-monitoring, but right now I feel like I'm losing my cognitive grip.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been experiencing periods of persistent irritability, elevated mood, or an increased sense of energy along with these racing thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been swinging between feeling almost euphoric and invincible, and then getting irrationally snappy with my wife and colleagues over minor things. At the same time, I feel this relentless drive to keep producing, taking on extra clinic work and starting multiple projects at all hours because I just can't sit still.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how long you've been experiencing these episodes of racing thoughts, elevated mood, and increased energy, and whether they come and go or have been persistent over time?\n\nPatient: This entire experience has been unfolding over the past few months, but it has shifted. The intense, high-energy surge with the racing thoughts and elevated mood was a temporary phase that has since passed, whereas this heavier, flatter, and guilt-ridden state has been persistently hanging over me for about the last four months.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if there were distinct periods where you experienced the high-energy, elevated mood state without the depressive symptoms, and if so, how long those periods typically lasted before shifting to the current state?\n\nPatient: Yes, there was a very distinct, highly driven period that started right after I returned from a stimulating conference, and that intense, elevated state lasted for about a month without any depression. Once that surge faded, I transitioned directly into this heavy, flat state which has now been dominant for the last four months.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many days or weeks the elevated mood and high-energy state lasted before you transitioned into the depressive state, and have you experienced any similar episodes of elevated mood in the past?\n\nPatient: The elevated, high-energy phase lasted for about a month after I returned from my conference, right before I transitioned into this depressive state that has now been going on for about four months. I have never experienced an episode like this in the past, as I've always prided myself on maintaining a careful balance and self-monitoring.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the month-long period of elevated mood and high energy was completely free of depressive symptoms, and did it meet a full two weeks of duration with at least three symptoms of mania or hypomania?\n\nPatient: Actually, looking back, that high-energy month wasn't completely free of depressive symptoms; those flat feelings and dark thoughts have been looming for about four months, overlapping even as that surge came and went. That intense phase lasted well over two weeks—about a month—and definitely involved multiple symptoms like my racing thoughts, pressured speech, insomnia, and taking on far too many activities.\n\nDoctor: Did the month-long period of elevated mood and high energy significantly impact your daily life or relationships in a noticeable way, such as requiring hospitalization or causing problems at work or with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has severely disrupted both my career and my marriage. At the clinic, I was pulled from leading my new program and had to transfer several patients after concerns were raised about my clinical judgment. At home, my wife feels like she is living with a stranger who is constantly working, pacing, or arguing instead of being present with her.\n\nDoctor: Can you further clarify if there were any periods where you experienced depressive symptoms concurrently with the manic or hypomanic symptoms, or if these states occurred distinctly separate from one another?\n\nPatient: The states actually overlapped significantly, as the low mood, loss of interest, and darker thoughts have been present for about four months, persisting even during that high-energy surge. Even while running on high energy, I felt a bone-deep fatigue underneath and intense guilt if I slowed down for a moment, though the humming voice I heard only occurred during those peak manic periods.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing a humming voice during the peak manic periods, can you tell me more about this experience, such as what the voice said or how it made you feel?\n\nPatient: In my closed office, I would hear what sounded like a woman humming a specific tune and occasionally saying my name, and it felt like an actual, physical sound in the room rather than just a thought. It was incredibly unsettling to experience as a psychologist, but in those highly wired moments, I felt convinced the voice was guiding me on what to do next with my patients. This only occurred when my mood was ramped up and my sleep had collapsed, disappearing completely once I crashed into this low state.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the humming voice you heard during the manic periods was the only psychotic symptom you experienced, or were there any other hallucinations or delusions present during that time or at any other point?\n\nPatient: No, the humming voice wasn't the only thing; I also felt convinced that I was receiving special insights and instructions from artwork and song lyrics to guide my treatment decisions. Both the voice and those symbolic insights occurred exclusively during those highly wired, sleep-deprived manic periods. Once I crashed into this lower, depressed state, those experiences completely disappeared.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever lose touch with reality or have difficulty distinguishing between the humming voice, special insights, and your own thoughts during the manic periods, or were you always aware that these experiences were unusual and not part of everyday reality?\n\nPatient: During those manic periods, I completely lost touch with reality and genuinely believed those experiences were real, feeling convinced that the humming was an actual sound in the room and that the insights from art were truly guiding my clinical decisions. It was only after a trusted colleague confronted me about a risky decision and my mood began to shift that I had the distressing realization that I had lost touch with reality and could no longer trust my own judgment.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if the depressive symptoms you've been experiencing for the last four months have included any psychotic symptoms, such as hallucinations or delusions, similar to those you experienced during the manic periods?\n\nPatient: No, those psychotic symptoms have not occurred at all during the depressive periods. They only happen when my mood is highly ramped up and my sleep is completely disrupted. During these low, exhausted, and guilty stretches, the voice and the special insights disappear completely.\n\nDoctor: Did the presence of psychotic symptoms, such as the humming voice and special insights, only occur during the manic or hypomanic episodes, or were they also present during any periods of depression?\n\nPatient: Yes, the humming voice and the special insights only occurred during the high-energy, wired periods when my mood was ramped up and my sleep fell apart. They faded away completely during the low, exhausted, and guilty stretches, and have not been present at all during my depression.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that the psychotic symptoms, like the humming voice and special insights, only occurred during the manic periods and not during the depressive episodes. Can you confirm if these psychotic symptoms were present for the majority of the time during those manic periods, or were they episodic and occurring only at the peak of your manic symptoms?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure about the exact proportion of time. I can't really confirm if it was the majority of the time or just the absolute peak, but those experiences definitely seemed tied to when my mind was racing the fastest and my sleep was at its worst.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the psychotic symptoms, such as the humming voice and special insights, were directly related to the intensity of your manic symptoms, and did they disappear completely when you transitioned into the depressive state?\n\nPatient: Yes, I can confirm that. The humming voice and those special insights only occurred when my mood was highly elevated and my sleep was at its worst. Once I transitioned into this low, drained, and guilty state, those experiences faded away completely.\n\nDoctor: Did the presence of psychotic symptoms during the manic periods influence your diagnosis, considering they are not present during the depressive episodes, and how do you think this distinction affects your overall condition?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
As a psychologist, I recognize that this distinction is clinically crucial because it confirms the psychotic features are tied directly to my manic episodes rather than indicating a continuous, primary psychotic disorder. The humming voice and special insights only happened when I was highly wired and sleep-deprived, disappearing completely once I transitioned into this low, depressive state. Understanding that these symptoms are bound to the mania helps me make sense of a Bipolar I diagnosis and gives me some much-needed clarity during a very disorienting time.

Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol). Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
